    69	You can contribute to the translation effort on our `crowdin project <https://crowdin.com/project/sepal-ui>`__.
    70	
    71	--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    72	
    73	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows.
    74	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.
    75	
    76	The full documentation is available `here <https://sepal-ui.readthedocs.io/en/latest/>`__ and demo apps can be launched on Heroku following these links:
    77	
    78	-   `Map style application <https://sepal-map-app.herokuapp.com>`__
    79	-   `Panel style application <https://sepal-panel-app.herokuapp.com>`__
    80	
    81	We are happy to receive feedback and we welcome any kind of contribution.
    82	
    83	+-----------+-------------+
    84	| |map-app| | |panel-app| |
    85	+-----------+-------------+
    86	
    87	.. |map-app| image:: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/12rambau/sepal_ui/main/docs/source/_image/demo-map-app.png
    88	    :width: 100%
    89	
    90	.. |panel-app| image:: https://raw.githubusercontent.com/12rambau/sepal_ui/main/docs/source/_image/demo-panel-app.png
    91	    :width: 100%

P: 1
R: 1
O:1
B:2
A:1
I:2
L:1
T:1

So the letters with duplicates are B and I, each appearing twice. The rest are unique. So when forming four-letter strings, I need to consider cases where there are duplicates. Since the original word has letters that can repeat up to 2 times, but when forming the string, we can't exceed those counts. 

So, the problem becomes counting the number of distinct four-letter arrangements considering the multiplicities of the letters. This sounds like a permutations of multiset problem. The general formula for permutations of a multiset is n! divided by the product of the factorials of the counts of each distinct element. But here, the situation is a bit different because we are selecting 4 letters out of the available ones, not using all of them. So we can't directly apply the multiset permutation formula. Instead, we need to consider different cases based on how many duplicate letters are used in the four-letter string.

First, let's note that the available letters are: P, R, O, B, A, B, I, L, I, T, Y. So total letters: 11. But with duplicates in B and I. So when forming a four-letter string, we have to make sure that we don't use more than 2 Bs or 2 Is.

Therefore, the possible cases for the four-letter strings are:

1. All four letters are distinct.
2. One pair of identical letters and two distinct letters.
3. Two pairs of identical letters.
4. Three of a kind and one distinct letter.
5. Four of a kind.

1. All distinct letters.

Hmm, this approach isn't working. The formula must be incorrect.

So let's try to think differently.

The key idea is, for all primes, the total LCM is product of maximal contributions from each of their copies such that each component' contribution is at most p_i's maximum possible, and use this to maximize LCM.

The way to maximize the LCM is as follows:

For each prime factor p appearing multiple times in the list, the first occurrence is chosen to give period p, then the others are chosen to contribute a period of (p-1), so that their LCM is p*(p-1).

For each occurrence of p in the list：

- The period can be either p or a factor of (p-1).

- Choosing one of these to be p, and the rest to be as many as possible.

Thus for the component contributions:

Each occurrence can contribute factors of the primes in its period's factors.

To maximize LCM:

- One needs to choose, for each duplicate of p, a period that introduces primes not in other terms' periods.

 However, the optimal choice for duplicates is to contribute p-1, because p-1 can potentially have different prime factors than p.

 For example, p is an odd prime, p的 factors are in p's own primes and p-1's.

 Thus:

 the first occurrence of p gives a factor of p.

 the other occurrences contribute the numbers which multiply the LCM with the factors of (p-1).

 The maximum possible contribution from duplicates is to include all the factors of p-1. Therefore, by setting one's period to p-1, the LCM will include those factors.

 Since the LCM of p and p-1 is p*(p-1).

 Thus, for each unique prime p, the maximum contribution from all its occurrences in the list is LCM(p, p-1).

 The total LCM is the product of these LCM(p,p-1) for each unique prime in the list.

 But this again conflicts with the first example:

 First example primes are 2,3,5,7.

 Their LCM contributions are:

2 &1 → LCM 2.

</think>To determine the number of distinct strings of four letters that can be formed from the letters of "probability," we need to consider the frequencies of the letters and the different cases of letter selection. The letters in "probability" are P, R, O, B, A, B, I, L, I, T, Y with frequencies: B (2), I (2), and the rest (P, R, O, A, L, T, Y) each occurring once.

We consider three cases:

1. **All four letters are distinct**:
   - There are 9 distinct letters: P, R, O, B, A, I, L, T, Y.
   - The number of ways to choose 4 distinct letters from these 9 is given by the combination \( \binom{9}{4} \).
   - Each selection of 4 letters can be arranged in \( 4! \) ways.
   - Therefore, the total number of distinct strings in this case is:
     \[
     \binom{9}{4} \times 4! = 126 \times 24 = 3024
     \]

2. **One pair of identical letters and two distinct letters**:
   - The pairs can be either B or I.
   - For each pair (BB or II), we need to choose 2 distinct letters from the remaining 8 letters (excluding the pair's letter).
   - The number of ways to choose 2 distinct letters from 8 is \( \binom{8}{2} \).
   - Each selection of 4 letters (with one pair and two distinct letters) can be arranged in \( \frac{4!}{2!} \) ways (since the pair is indistinguishable).
   - Therefore, the total number of distinct strings for each pair is:
     \[
     \binom{8}{2} \times \frac{4!}{2!} = 28 \times 12 = 336
     \]
   - Since there are two possible pairs (BB and II), the total for this case is:
     \[
     2 \times 336 = 672
     \]

3. **Two pairs of identical letters**:
   - The only possible pairs are BB and II.
   - The number of ways to arrange these 4 letters (BB and II) is given by:
     \[
     \frac{4!}{2! \times 2!} = 6
     \]

Summing up all cases:
- Case 1: 3024
- Case 2: 672
- Case 3: 6

The total number of distinct strings is:
\[
3024 + 672 + 6 = 3702
\]

Therefore, the number of distinct strings of four letters that can be formed from the letters of "probability" is:
\[

<think>
Okay, let's try to tackle this problem. Hmm, so we have a function f defined on [0, ∞) by f(x) = e^{m + sqrt(x)} + e^{m - sqrt(x)}, where m is a natural number. Then, L_n is the limit as x approaches 0 from the positive side of e^{-m} times the nth derivative of f at x. We need to find L_{2002}. 

Alright, first, let's parse this. The function f combines two exponential terms with sqrt(x) in the exponent. The derivatives of f at x, then multiplied by e^{-m}, and then take the limit as x approaches 0. The question is about the 2002nd derivative, which is a very high order. But since we're taking the limit as x approaches 0, maybe there's a pattern or a simplification that occurs when x is near 0, which could make higher derivatives manageable.

First, maybe it's helpful to rewrite f(x) in a way that factors out e^m. Let's see:

f(x) = e^{m + sqrt(x)} + e^{m - sqrt(x)} = e^m (e^{sqrt(x)} + e^{-sqrt(x)}) = e^m * 2 * cosh(sqrt(x)). 

Oh, right! Because cosh(t) = (e^t + e^{-t}) / 2. So f(x) = 2 e^m cosh(sqrt(x)). That seems simpler. So then, when we multiply by e^{-m}, we get e^{-m} f(x) = 2 cosh(sqrt(x)). 

Therefore, L_n is the limit as x approaches 0+ of the nth derivative of 2 cosh(sqrt(x)) evaluated at x, right? Because we can interchange the e^{-m} with taking derivatives, since e^{-m} is a constant. So L_n = 2 * lim_{x→0+} [d^n/dx^n cosh(sqrt(x))].

So the problem reduces to finding the nth derivative of cosh(sqrt(x)) evaluated at x approaching 0. Let's focus on that. Let’s let’s denote g(x) = cosh(sqrt(x)). We need to find the nth derivative of g(x) and then the limit as x approaches 0+.

So that simplifies to Σ_{k=0}^∞ x^k / (2k)!.

So the Taylor series expansion of g(x) = cosh(sqrt(x)) is Σ_{k=0}^∞ x^k / (2k)!.

Therefore, around x=0, all the derivatives of g(x) can be found by looking at the coefficients of this series. Therefore, the nth derivative of g at 0 is n! / (2n)!.

</think>To solve the problem, we start by rewriting the function \( f(x) = e^{m + \sqrt{x}} + e^{m - \sqrt{x}} \) in a more convenient form. Notice that:
\[
f(x) = e^{m + \sqrt{x}} + e^{m - \sqrt{x}} = e^m \left( e^{\sqrt{x}} + e^{-\sqrt{x}} \right) = 2 e^m \cosh(\sqrt{x}).
\]
Multiplying by \( e^{-m} \), we get:
\[
e^{-m} f(x) = 2 \cosh(\sqrt{x}).
\]
We need to find the limit \( L_n = \lim_{x \to 0, x > 0} e^{-m} f^{(n)}(x) \), which simplifies to:
\[
L_n = 2 \lim_{x \to 0^+} \frac{d^n}{dx^n} \cosh(\sqrt{x}).
\]

Next, we use the Taylor series expansion of \( \cosh(\sqrt{x}) \) around \( x = 0 \):
\[
\cosh(\sqrt{x}) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \frac{x^k}{(2k)!}.
\]
The \( n \)-th derivative of \( \cosh(\sqrt{x}) \) evaluated at \( x = 0 \) is given by the coefficient of \( x^n \) in the Taylor series, multiplied by \( n! \). The coefficient of \( x^n \) in the series is \( \frac{1}{(2n)!} \). Therefore, the \( n \)-th derivative of \( \cosh(\sqrt{x}) \) at \( x = 0 \) is:
\[
\left. \frac{d^n}{dx^n} \cosh(\sqrt{x}) \right|_{x=0} = \frac{n!}{(2n)!}.
\]
Thus, the limit \( L_n \) is:
\[
L_n = 2 \cdot \frac{n!}{(2n)!}.
\]

For \( n = 2002 \), we have:
\[
L_{2002} = 2 \cdot \frac{2002!}{4004!}.
\]

Therefore, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{2 \cdot 2002!}{4004!}}.

Vector CG: G - C = (1 - sqrt(3)/2 - 0, 0.5 - 1) = (1 - sqrt(3)/2, -0.5)

Vector CB: B - C = (1 - 0, 0 - 1) = (1, -1)

Compute the angle between vectors CG and CB using the dot product formula:

cosθ = (CG · CB) / (|CG| |CB|)

First, compute the dot product:

CG · CB = (1 - sqrt(3)/2)(1) + (-0.5)(-1) = 1 - sqrt(3)/2 + 0.5 = 1.5 - sqrt(3)/2

|CB| = sqrt(1^2 + (-1)^2) = sqrt(2)

Simplify denominator:

sqrt(2) * sqrt(2 - sqrt(3)) = sqrt(2*(2 - sqrt(3))) = sqrt(4 - 2*sqrt(3))

So cosθ ≈ 0.634 / 0.732 ≈ 0.866

Which is approximately sqrt(3)/2 ≈ 0.866. So θ ≈ 30 degrees.

To solve this problem, we need to determine whether a given integer \( a \) is a perfect square. If it is not a perfect square, we output 1; otherwise, we output 0.

### Approach
The approach involves checking if the given integer \( a \) has an integer square root. This can be done efficiently by computing the integer square root of \( a \) and then squaring it to see if it equals the original number. 

1. **Compute the Integer Square Root**: We use the `math.isqrt` function to compute the integer square root of \( a \). This function returns the largest integer less than or equal to the square root of \( a \).
2. **Check Equality**: We then check if squaring this integer square root gives back the original number \( a \). If it does, \( a \) is a perfect square, and we output 0. If not, \( a \) is not a perfect square, and we output 1.

### Solution Code
```python
import math

a = int(input())
root = math.isqrt(a)
print(1 if root * root != a else 0)
```

### Explanation
1. **Reading Input**: The input integer \( a \) is read.
2. **Integer Square Root**: `math.isqrt(a)` computes the integer square root of \( a \). For example, for \( a = 129 \), the integer square root is 11 because \( 11^2 = 121 \), which is the largest square less than 129.
3. **Check Condition**: We then square the integer square root. If it equals \( a \), \( a \) is a perfect square, so we print 0. Otherwise, we print 1.

This approach efficiently determines whether \( a \) is a perfect square in \( O(1) \) time complexity, leveraging the mathematical properties of square numbers.<#sep#>alt: Documentation Status

\[
G = \left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \frac{1}{2}\right)
\]

To find \( \angle GCB \), we calculate the vectors \( \overrightarrow{CG} \) and \( \overrightarrow{CB} \):
\[
\overrightarrow{CG} = \left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - 0, \frac{1}{2} - 1\right) = \left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}\right)
\]
\[
\overrightarrow{CB} = (1 - 0, 0 - 1) = (1, -1)
\]

The dot product of \( \overrightarrow{CG} \) and \( \overrightarrow{CB} \) is:
\[
\overrightarrow{CG} \cdot \overrightarrow{CB} = \left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) \cdot 1 + \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) \cdot (-1) = 1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2} - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

The magnitudes of \( \overrightarrow{CG} \) and \( \overrightarrow{CB} \) are:
\[
|\overrightarrow{CG}| = \sqrt{\left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^2 + \left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\left(1 - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{4}}
\]
\[
= \sqrt{1 - \sqrt{3} + \frac{3}{4} + \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{2 - \sqrt{3}}
\]
\[
|\overrightarrow{CB}| = \sqrt{1^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{2}
\]

The cosine of the angle \( \theta \) between \( \overrightarrow{CG} \) and \( \overrightarrow{CB} \) is:
\[
\cos \theta = \frac{\overrightarrow{CG} \cdot \overrightarrow{CB}}{|\overrightarrow{CG}| |\overrightarrow{CB}|} = \frac{\frac{3}{2} - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}}{\sqrt{2 - \sqrt{3}} \cdot \sqrt{2}} = \frac{\frac{3 - \sqrt{3}}{2}}{\sqrt{4 - 2\sqrt{3}}}
\]
\[
= \frac{\frac{3 - \sqrt{3}}{2}}{\sqrt{(2 - \sqrt{3})^2}} = \frac{\frac{3 - \sqrt{3}}{2}}{2 - \sqrt{3}} = \frac{3 - \sqrt{3}}{2(2 - \sqrt{3})} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

Thus, \( \theta = \arccos\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) = 30^\circ \).

Therefore, the angle \( \angle GCB \) is \(\boxed{30^\circ}\).<#sep#>human: Roy and Biv have a set of n points on the infinite number line.

Each point has one of 3 colors: red, green, or blue.

Roy and Biv would like to connect all the points with some edges. Edges can be drawn between any of the two of the given points. The cost of an edge is equal to the distance between the two points it connects.

They want to do this in such a way that they will both see that all the points are connected (either directly or indirectly).

However, there is a catch: Roy cannot see the color red and Biv cannot see the color blue.

Therefore, they have to choose the edges in such a way that if all the red points are removed, the remaining blue and green points are connected (and similarly, if all the blue points are removed, the remaining red and green points are connected).

Help them compute the minimum cost way to choose edges to satisfy the above constraints.
Input

The first line will contain an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 300 000), the number of points.

The next n lines will contain two tokens pi and ci (pi is an integer, 1 ≤ pi ≤ 109, ci is a uppercase English letter 'R', 'G' or 'B'), denoting the position of the i-th point and the color of the i-th point. 'R' means red, 'G' denotes green, and 'B' means blue. The positions will be in strictly increasing order.

Output

Print a single integer, the minimum cost way to solve the problem.

Examples

Input

4
1 G
5 R
10 B
15 G


Output

23


Input

4
1 G
2 R
3 B
10 G


Output

12

Note

In the first sample, it is optimal to draw edges between the points (1,2), (1,4), (3,4). These have costs 4, 14, 5, respectively.

   162	        Check if the date is provided using the date format required for the widget.
   163	
   164	        Args:
   165	            date: the date to test in YYYY-MM-DD format

   167	        Returns:
   168	            The validity of the date with respect to the datepicker format

   173	        except (ValueError, TypeError):
   174	            valid = False

   319	    def reset(self, *args) -> Self:
   320	        """
   321	        Clear the File selection and move to the root folder.
   322	        """
   323	        # note: The args arguments are useless here but need to be kept so that
   324	        # the function is natively compatible with the clear btn
   325	
   326	        # do nothing if nothing is set to avoids extremelly long waiting
   327	        # time when multiple fileInput are reset at the same time as in the aoiView
   328	        if self.v_model is not None:
   329	
   330	            # move to root
   331	            self._on_file_select({"new": Path.home()})
   332	
   333	            # remove v_model
   334	            self.v_model = ""
   335	
   336	        return self
   337	
   338	    def select_file(self, path: Union[str, Path]) -> Self:
   339	        """
   340	        Manually select a file from it's path. No verification on the extension is performed.
   341	
   342	        Args:
   343	            path: the path to the file
   344	        """
   345	        # cast to Path
   346	        path = Path(path)
   347	
   348	        # test file existence
   349	        if not path.is_file():
   350	            raise Exception(f"{path} is not a file")
   351	
   352	        # set the menu to the folder of the file
   353	        self._on_file_select({"new": path.parent})
   354	
   355	        # select the appropriate file
   356	        self._on_file_select({"new": path})
   357	
   358	        return self
   359	
   360	    def _on_file_select(self, change: dict) -> Self:
   361	        """
   362	        Dispatch the behavior between file selection and folder change.
   363	        """
   364	        if not change["new"]:
   365	            return self
   366	
   367	        new_value = Path(change["new"])
   368	
   369	        if new_value.is_dir():
   370	            self.folder = new_value
   371	            self._change_folder()
   372	
   373	        elif new_value.is_file():
   374	            self.file = str(new_value)
   375	
   376	        return self
   377	
   378	    @sd.switch("indeterminate", on_widgets=["loading"])
   379	    def _change_folder(self) -> None:
   380	        """
   381	        Change the target folder.
   382	        """
   383	        # get the items
   384	        items = self._get_items()
   385	
   386	        # reset files
   387	        # this is reseting the scroll to top without using js scripts
   388	        self.file_list.children[0].children = []
   389	
   390	        # set the new files
   391	        self.file_list.children[0].children = items
   392	
   393	        return
   394	
   395	    def _get_items(self) -> List[v.ListItem]:
   396	        """
   397	        Create the list of items inside the folder.
   398	
   399	        Returns:
   400	            list of items inside the selected folder
   401	        """
   402	        folder = self.folder
   403	
   404	        list_dir = [el for el in folder.glob("*/") if not el.name.startswith(".")]
   405	
   406	        if self.extentions:
   407	            list_dir = [
   408	                el for el in list_dir if el.is_dir() or el.suffix in self.extentions
   409	            ]
   410	
   411	        folder_list = []
   412	        file_list = []
   413	
   414	        for el in list_dir:
   415	
   416	            if el.is_dir():
   417	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["icon"]
   418	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   419	            elif el.suffix in self.ICON_STYLE.keys():
   420	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["icon"]
   421	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["color"][<#sep#> function itself is a power function.

Therefore, I think the only solutions are sequences where a_n is a fixed power of n. Hence, the answer is that all such sequences are of the form a_n =n^k for some fixed positive integer k.

To confirm, let's check if there's any other structure. Suppose someone tries to construct a sequence where a_2=2, a_4=4, a_3=3, a_9=9, etc., which is a_n =n. This works. If a_2=4, a_4=16, a_8=64, a_3=9, a_9=81, etc., which is a_n =n^2. So these work. But if you tried to mix, like set a_2=2, a_3=3^2=9, then a_4= (a_2)^2=4, but 9 >4, which would break the strictly increasing requirement. So you can't mix exponents for different primes. Therefore, the exponent must be the same across all primes, leading to a_n =n^k.

Hence, the conclusion is that the only strictly increasing sequences {a_n} satisfying the given condition are the power sequences a_n =n^k for some fixed positive integer k.

**Final Answer**
The sequences are all positive integer powers, so the solutions are \boxed{a_n = n^k} for some fixed positive integer \( k \).
</think>To find all strictly increasing sequences \(\{a_n\}\) of positive integers that satisfy \(a_{n^i} = a_n^i\) for all positive integers \(i\), we start by analyzing the given condition.

First, let's consider the form \(a_n = n^k\) for some fixed positive integer \(k\). We check if this form satisfies the condition:
- For \(a_n = n^k\), we have:
  \[
  a_{n^i} = (n^i)^k = n^{ki}
  \]
- On the other hand:
  \[
  (a_n)^i = (n^k)^i = n^{ki}
  \]
Since both expressions are equal, \(a_n = n^k\) satisfies the condition \(a_{n^i} = a_n^i\).

Next, we need to verify that no other forms of sequences can satisfy the condition while maintaining strict monotonicity. Suppose there exists a sequence \(\{a_n\}\) that is not of the form \(a_n = n^k\). For the sequence to be strictly increasing, each term must be larger than the previous one. Additionally, the given condition \(a_{n^i} = a_n^i\) imposes a strong multiplicative structure on the sequence.

Consider the prime factorization of \(n\). If \(n = p_1^{m_1} p_2^{m_2} \cdots p_r^{m_r}\), where \(p_i\) are primes, then:
- For \(a_n\) to be consistent with the given condition, it must be multiplicative, i.e., \(a_n = a_{p_1^{m_1}} \cdot a_{p_2^{m_2}} \cdots a_{p_r^{m_r}}\).
- For prime powers, \(a_{p^m} = (a_p)^m\).

To maintain strict monotonicity, the exponents assigned to different primes must be consistent. If \(a_p = p^k\) for all primes \(p\), then:
- For composite numbers \(n = p_1^{m_1} p_2^{m_2} \cdots p_r^{m_r}\):
  \[
  a_n = (p_1^{m_1})^k \cdot (p_2^{m_2})^k \cdots (p_r^{m_r})^k = (p_1^{m_1} p_2^{m_2} \cdots p_r^{m_r})^k = n^k
  \]
This shows that the sequence \(a_n = n^k\) is the only form that satisfies both the given condition and the strict monotonicity requirement.

Therefore, the only strictly increasing sequences \(\{a_n\}\) of positive integers that satisfy \(a_{n^i} = a_n^i\) for all positive integers \(i\) are of the form:
\[
\boxed{a_n = n^k}
\]

Fourth factor: a² + b² + c² + d⁶

Now, if we can apply AM-GM to each of these factors. Let's see. The AM-GM inequality states that for non-negative real numbers, the arithmetic mean is greater than or equal to the geometric mean, with equality when all numbers are equal.

But each factor is a sum of three squares and one sixth power. Let's see. For example, first factor: a⁶ + b² + c² + d². If we try to relate this to the geometric mean, perhaps.

But since the exponents are different, maybe we can use weighted AM-GM. Let's try to apply AM-GM on each term:

For the first factor: a⁶ + b² + c² + d².

Suppose we consider the terms as a⁶, b², c², d². To apply AM-GM, we have four terms. The geometric mean would be (a⁶ * b² * c² * d²)^(1/4). The arithmetic mean is (a⁶ + b² + c² + d²)/4.

By AM-GM: (a⁶ + b² + c² + d²)/4 ≥ (a⁶ b² c² d²)^(1/4)

Multiply both sides by 4: a⁶ + b² + c² + d² ≥ 4(a⁶ b² c² d²)^(1/4)

Similarly, for the other three factors:

Second factor: a² + b⁶ + c² + d² ≥4(a² b⁶ c² d²)^(1/4)

Third factor: a² + b² + c⁶ + d² ≥4(a² b² c⁶ d²)^(1/4)

Fourth factor: a² + b² + c² + d⁶ ≥4(a² b² c² d⁶)^(1/4)

Multiplying all four inequalities together:

Product of RHS factors ≥ 4^4 * [ (a⁶ b² c² d²)(a² b⁶ c² d²)(a² b² c⁶ d²)(a² b² c² d⁶) ]^(1/4)

Let's compute the product inside the brackets:

Multiply the four terms:

(a⁶ b² c² d²) * (a² b⁶ c² d²) * (a² b² c⁶ d²) * (a² b² c² d⁶)

For a: 6 + 2 +2 +2 =12

For b:2 +6 +2 +2=12

For c:2 +2 +6 +2=12

For d:2 +2 +2 +6=12

So the product is a^12 b^12 c^12 d^12.

Therefore, [a^12 b^12 c^12 d^12]^(1/4) = (a^12 b^12 c^12 d^12)^(1/4) = a^3 b^3 c^3 d^3.

Therefore, the product of the four inequalities gives:

Product of RHS factors ≥ 256 a^3 b^3 c^3 d^3

    73	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows.
    74	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.
    75	
    76	The full documentation is available `here <https://sepal-ui.readthedocs.io/en/latest/>`__ and demo apps can be launched on Heroku following these links:
    77	
    78	-   `Map style application <https://sepal-map-app.herokuapp.com>`__
    79	-   `Panel style application <https://sepal-panel-app.herokuapp.com>`__
    80	
    81	We are happy to receive feedback and we welcome any kind of contribution.

</think>To solve the equation

\[
256a^3b^3c^3d^3 = (a^6 + b^2 + c^2 + d^2)(a^2 + b^6 + c^2 + d^2)(a^2 + b^2 + c^6 + d^2)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + d^6),
\]

we will determine all quadruples \((a, b, c, d)\) of real numbers that satisfy this equation.

### Step 1: Trivial Solution
First, consider the case where \(a = b = c = d = 0\):
\[
256 \cdot 0^3 \cdot 0^3 \cdot 0^3 \cdot 0^3 = 0,
\]
and
\[
(0^6 + 0^2 + 0^2 + 0^2)(0^2 + 0^6 + 0^2 + 0^2)(0^2 + 0^2 + 0^6 + 0^2)(0^2 + 0^2 + 0^2 + 0^6) = 0.
\]
Both sides are zero, so \((0, 0, 0, 0)\) is a solution.

### Step 2: Non-Trivial Solutions
Assume \(a, b, c, d\) are not all zero. We will use the Arithmetic Mean-Geometric Mean (AM-GM) inequality to analyze the equation.

#### Applying AM-GM Inequality
For each factor on the right-hand side (RHS), we apply the AM-GM inequality:
\[
a^6 + b^2 + c^2 + d^2 \geq 4 \sqrt[4]{a^6 b^2 c^2 d^2},
\]
\[
a^2 + b^6 + c^2 + d^2 \geq 4 \sqrt[4]{a^2 b^6 c^2 d^2},
\]
\[
a^2 + b^2 + c^6 + d^2 \geq 4 \sqrt[4]{a^2 b^2 c^6 d^2},
\]
\[
a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + d^6 \geq 4 \sqrt[4]{a^2 b^2 c^2 d^6}.
\]

Multiplying these inequalities together, we get:
\[
(a^6 + b^2 + c^2 + d^2)(a^2 + b^6 + c^2 + d^2)(a^2 + b^2 + c^6 + d^2)(a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + d^6) \geq 256 (a^3 b^3 c^3 d^3).
\]

Equality holds if and only if all terms in each factor are equal. This implies:
\[
a^6 = b^2 = c^2 = d^2.
\]

### Step 3: Solving for \(a, b, c, d\)
From \(a^6 = b^2 = c^2 = d^2\), we have:
\[
a^6 = b^2 \implies b = \pm a^3,
\]
\[
a^6 = c^2 \implies c = \pm a^3,
\]
\[
a^6 = d^2 \implies d = \pm a^3.
\]

Since \(a^6 = a^2\), we have:
\[
a^6 - a^2 = 0 \implies a^2(a^4 - 1) = 0 \implies a^2 = 0 \text{ or } a^4 = 1.
\]

Thus, \(a = 0\) or \(a = \pm 1\).

#### Case 1: \(a = 0\)
If \(a = 0\), then \(b = c = d = 0\), which we already considered.

#### Case 2: \(a = \pm 1\)
If \(a = 1\), then \(b, c, d = \pm 1\). Similarly, if \(a = -1\), then \(b, c, d = \pm 1\).

### Step 4: Verifying Solutions
We need to ensure that the product \(a^3 b^3 c^3 d^3\) is positive. This requires an even number of negative entries among \(a, b, c, d\).

Thus, the solutions are:
\[
(0, 0, 0, 0) \quad \text{and} \quad (\pm 1, \pm 1, \pm 1, \pm 1) \text{ with an even number of negative entries}.
\]

### Final Answer
The solutions are:

\[
\boxed{(0, 0, 0, 0) \text{ and all quadruples } (\pm 1, \pm 1, \pm 1, \pm 1) \text{ with an even number of negative entries}}
\]<#sep#>human: For a given weighted directed graph G(V, E), find the distance of the shortest route that meets the following criteria:

* It is a closed cycle where it ends at the same point it starts.
* It visits each vertex exactly once.

Constraints

* 2 ≤ |V| ≤ 15
* 0 ≤ di ≤ 1,000
* There are no multiedge
Input


|V| |E|
s0 t0 d0
s1 t1 d1
:
s|E|-1 t|E|-1 d|E|-1


|V| is the number of vertices and |E| is the number of edges in the graph. The graph vertices are named with the numbers 0, 1,..., |V|-1 respectively.

si and ti represent source and target vertices of i-th edge (directed) and di represents the distance between si and ti (the i-th edge).

Output

Print the shortest distance in a line. If there is no solution, print -1.

Examples

Input

4 6
0 1 2
1 2 3
1 3 9
2 0 1
2 3 6
3 2 4


Output

16


Input

3 3
0 1 1
1 2 1
0 2 1


Output

-1

     1	"""
     2	All the process that can be run using nox.
     3	
     4	The nox run are build in isolated environment that will be stored in .nox. to force the venv update, remove the .nox/xxx folder.
     5	"""
     6	
     7	import nox
     8	
     9	
    10	@nox.session(reuse_venv=True)
    11	def lint(session):
    12	    """Apply the pre-commits."""
    13	    session.install("pre-commit")
    14	    session.run("pre-commit", "run", "--a", *session.posargs)
    15	
    16	
    17	@nox.session(reuse_venv=True)
    18	def test(session):
    19	    """Run all the test using the environment varialbe of the running machine."""
    20	    session.install(".[test]")
    21	    test_files = session.posargs or ["tests"]
    22	    session.run("pytest", "--color=yes", *test_files)
    23	
    24	
    25	@nox.session(reuse_venv=True)
    26	def bin(session):
    27	    """Run all the bin methods to validate the conda recipe."""
    28	    session.install(".")
    29	    session.run("module_deploy", "--help")
    30	    session.run("module_factory", "--help")
    31	    session.run("module_l10n", "--help")
    32	    session.run("module_theme", "--help")
    33	    session.run("module_venv", "--help")
    34	    session.run("activate_venv", "--help")
    35	    session.run("sepal_ipyvuetify", "--help")
    36	
    37	
    38	@nox.session(reuse_venv=True)

(1 + d)^2 + (y + d)/(1 + d) = y + 1 + d.

Multiply both sides by (1 + d):

(1 + d)^3 + y + d = (y + 1 + d)(1 + d).

Expand the right side: y(1 + d) + (1 + d)^2.

Left side: (1 + 3d + 3d² + d³) + y + d.

Right side: y(1 + d) + 1 + 2d + d².

Set equal:

(1 + 3d + 3d² + d³) + y + d = y(1 + d) + 1 + 2d + d².

Left side: y + (1 + 3d + 3d² + d³ + d) = y + 1 + 4d + 3d² + d³.

Right side: y + y d + 1 + 2d + d².

Subtract right side from left side:

(y + 1 + 4d + 3d² + d³) - (y + y d + 1 + 2d + d²) = (0) + (2d) + (2d²) + d³ - y d = 0.

So we have (2d + 2d² + d³) - y d = 0 for all y ≠ -1. But this has to hold for all y ≠ -1, which is only possible if the coefficient of y is zero and the constant term is zero. So coefficient of y: -d = 0 => d = 0. Then the constant term: 2*0 + 2*0 + 0 = 0, which works. So if d = 0, then f(y) = y. Let's check this solution.

If f(y) = y, then c = f(1) = 1. Then the equation f(y + 1) = 1² + f(y)/1 => y + 1 = 1 + y, which holds. So f(y) = y is a solution. Let me check if this works in the original functional equation.

Original equation: f(x² + y) = f(x)² + f(xy)/f(x). If f(x) = x, then LHS is x² + y. RHS is x² + (xy)/x = x² + y. So yes, it works. So f(x) = x is a solution.

But maybe there are more solutions. Let's check if the assumption that f is linear is necessary. Alternatively, maybe f(x) = x is the only solution.

Alternatively, maybe there's a constant function. Suppose f(x) = c for all x ≠ 0, where c ≠ 0. Let's check if that works. Plug into the equation: c = c² + c/c = c² + 1. So c = c² + 1. Alternatively, maybe f(x) = x²? Let's test. Then LHS: f(x² + y) = (x² + y)^2. RHS: f(x)^2 + f(xy)/f(x) = (x²)^2 + ( (xy)^2 ) / (x²) ) = x⁴ + y². So (x² + y)^2 vs x⁴ + y². The left side is x⁴ + 2x² y + y². The right side is x⁴ + y². Therefore, unless 2x² y = 0, which isn't true for all y ≠ -x², this doesn't hold. So f(x) = x² is not a solution.

Hmm. What about f(x) = 1/x? Let's check. LHS: f(x² + y) = 1/(x² + y). RHS: f(x)^2 + f(xy)/f(x) = (1/x)^2 + (1/(xy))/(1/x) = 1/x² + x/(xy) = 1/x² + 1/y. So LHS is 1/(x² + y), RHS is 1/x² + 1/y. These are equal only if 1/(x² + y) = 1/x² + 1/y. Let's solve for y:

1/(x² + y) = (y + x²)/(x² y). Wait, cross multiplying: y = (x² + y) + x². Wait, that seems off. Let me compute:

Alternatively, the standard approach for bitmask DP with TSP is:

Initialize DP for a mask of a single node (any node).

    Wait, but this approach may process mask's in the wrong order. For instance, if a mask of length k is processed, and then masks of length k+1 are being processed, but the above loops over mask in numerical order. Which naturally does not ensure that lower masks are processed first. So when mask is a lower mask, but comes later in the numerical order, the transitions would not have been processed yet, so when computing mask, you might not have updated the new_mask before.

Thus, this approach might miss states because new_mask may have a bit count higher than the current mask, thus when the current mask is mask, and we're processing mask, when mask has a certain mask, e.g. 3 (bits 0 and 1 set) processed later than a mask with higher number of bits but smaller numerical value (e.g. 0b100, which is 4>3 but bit count is 1 <3's count 2. 

Thus, the transitions can't be properly made. 

Therefore, the masks must be processed in order of increasing bit count.

masks_ordered = []

Then, for each (bits, mask) in masks_ordered sorted by bits in increasing order:

   process the mask here.

   160	    def is_valid_date(date: str) -> bool:
   161	        """
   162	        Check if the date is provided using the date format required for the widget.
   163	
   164	        Args:

   167	        Returns:
   168	            The validity of the date with respect to the datepicker format

Set equal:

Coefficients of y²: a = a/k ⇒ k = 1 (assuming a ≠ 0).

Coefficients of y: 2a + b = b/k. If k=1, then 2a + b = b ⇒ 2a = 0 ⇒ a=0. But then quadratic term disappears. Contradiction. Therefore, quadratic functions don't work.

Hmm. So maybe the only solution is f(x) = x. Let's see if we can prove that.

From the case when x=1, we have f(y + 1) = c² + f(y)/c where c = f(1). If we can show that c must be 1, then perhaps f(y + 1) = 1 + f(y)/1 = f(y) + 1, which would lead to f(y) = y + d. But earlier, we saw that d must be zero. Alternatively, maybe from this recursion, f(y + 1) - f(y) = c² - (c - 1) f(y)/c. Wait, not sure.

Alternatively, let's consider the equation f(y + 1) - f(y)/c = c². Let me think of this as a linear recurrence. For a linear recurrence like f(y + 1) = a f(y) + b, the solution is f(y) = A a^y + b/(1 - a) if a ≠ 1. But here, y is a real number, not an integer, so the solution would need to be a function defined for all real y, which complicates things. However, if we can extend the solution to real numbers, maybe exponential functions. Suppose f(y) = A c^y + B. Let's try this. Then f(y + 1) = A c^{y + 1} + B. The right side is c² + (A c^y + B)/c. So:

A c^{y + 1} + B = c² + A c^{y - 1} + B/c.

Rearranged:

A c^{y + 1} - A c^{y - 1} + B - B/c - c² = 0.

Factor c^{y - 1}:

A c^{y - 1} (c² - 1) + B(1 - 1/c) - c² = 0.

For this to hold for all y ≠ -1, the coefficient of c^{y - 1} must be zero. So:

A(c² - 1) = 0.

And the constant term:

B(1 - 1/c) - c² = 0.

From the first equation, either A = 0 or c² - 1 = 0. If A = 0, then the equation becomes B(1 - 1/c) - c² = 0. If c ≠ 1, then B = c² / (1 - 1/c) = c³ / (c - 1). But then f(y) = B, a constant function, which we saw doesn't work. If c = 1, then c² - 1 = 0, so first equation holds. Then the constant term becomes B(1 - 1) - 1 = -1 = 0, which is a contradiction. Therefore, the only possibility is A ≠ 0 and c² - 1 = 0. Therefore, c² = 1 ⇒ c = 1 or c = -1.

Case 1: c = 1.

Then the constant term equation becomes B(1 - 1/1) - 1² = 0 ⇒ 0 - 1 = -1 = 0. Contradiction. Therefore, no solution here.

Case 2: c = -1.

Then the constant term equation becomes B(1 - 1/(-1)) - (-1)^2 = B(2) - 1 = 0 ⇒ B = 1/2.

Therefore, f(y) = A (-1)^y + 1/2. But since y is a real number, (-1)^y is not a real number unless y is integer. Therefore, this is not a valid function over the reals. Therefore, no solution here either.

Therefore, the only possible solution is when A = 0, which leads us back to constant functions, which we already saw don't work. Hence, the only solution that comes out of this is if c = 1, but that led to a contradiction unless f(y) is linear. Hence, f(y) = y is the only solution.

But maybe we need to check if there are other solutions not captured by these substitutions. Let's try to use the original equation again. Suppose we can set y such that x² + y = something. f(x(1 - x²)) = f(x)(f(1) - f(x)^2).

Let me denote f(x) as g(x). Then equation becomes g(x(1 - x²)) = g(x)(c - g(x)^2), where c = f(1). If we can find such a function g.

If g(x) = x, then g(x(1 - x²)) = x(1 - x²). On the other hand, g(x)(c - g(x)^2) = x(c - x²). For this to be equal, x(1 - x²) = x(c - x²). Assuming x ≠ 0, divide both sides by x: 1 - x² = c - x². Therefore, 1 = c. Therefore, c = 1. So this holds. Therefore, f(x) = x is a solution when c = 1.

   338	    def select_file(self, path: Union[str, Path]) -> Self:
   339	        """
   340	        Manually select a file from it's path. No verification on the extension is performed.
   341	
   342	        Args:
   343	            path: the path to the file
   344	        """
   345	        # cast to Path
   346	        path = Path(path)
   347	
   348	        # test file existence
   349	        if not path.is_file():
   350	            raise Exception(f"{path} is not a file")
   351	
   352	        # set the menu to the folder of the file
   353	        self._on_file_select({"new": path.parent})
   354	
   355	        # select the appropriate file
   356	        self._on_file_select({"new": path})
   357	
   358	        return self
   359	
   360	    def _on_file_select(self, change: dict) -> Self:
   361	        """
   362	        Dispatch the behavior between file selection and folder change.
   363	        """
   364	        if not change["new"]:
   365	            return self
   366	
   367	        new_value = Path(change["new"])
   368	
   369	        if new_value.is_dir():
   370	            self.folder = new_value
   371	            self._change_folder()
   372	
   373	        elif new_value.is_file():
   374	            self.file = str(new_value)
   375	
   376	        return self
   377	
   378	    @sd.switch("indeterminate", on_widgets=["loading"])
   379	    def _change_folder(self) -> None:
   380	        """
   381	        Change the target folder.
   382	        """
   383	        # get the items
   384	        items = self._get_items()
   385	
   386	        # reset files
   387	        # this is reseting the scroll to top without using js scripts
   388	        self.file_list.children[0].children = []
   389	
   390	        # set the new files
   391	        self.file_list.children[0].children = items
   392	
   393	        return
   394	
   395	    def _get_items(self) -> List[v.ListItem]:
   396	        """
   397	        Create the list of items inside the folder.
   398	
   399	        Returns:
   400	            list of items inside the selected folder
   401	        """
   402	        folder = self.folder
   403	
   404	        list_dir = [el for el in folder.glob("*/") if not el.name.startswith(".")]
   405	
   406	        if self.extentions:
   407	            list_dir = [
   408	                el for el in list_dir if el.is_dir() or el.suffix in self.extentions
   409	            ]
   410	
   411	        folder_list = []
   412	        file_list = []
   413	
   414	        for el in list_dir:
   415	
   416	            if el.is_dir():
   417	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["icon"]
   418	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[""]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   419	            elif el.suffix in self.ICON_STYLE.keys():
   420	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["icon"]
   421	                color = self.ICON_STYLE[el.suffix]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   422	            else:
   423	                icon = self.ICON_STYLE["DEFAULT"]["icon"]
   424	                color = self.ICON_STYLE["DEFAULT"]["color"][v.theme.dark]
   425	
   426	            children = [
   427	                v.ListItemAction(children=[v.Icon(color=color, children=[icon])]),
   428	                v.ListItemContent(
   429	                    children=[v.ListItemTitle(children=[el.stem + el.suffix])]
   430	                ),
   431	            ]
   432	
   433	            if el.is_dir():
   434	                folder_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   435	            else:
   436	                file_size = su.get_file_size(el)
   437	                children.append(
   438	                    v.ListItemActionText(class_="ml-1", children=[file_size])
   439	                )
   440	                file_list.append(v.ListItem(value=str(el), children=children))
   441	
   442	        folder_list = humansorted(folder_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   443	        file_list = humansorted(file_list, key=lambda x: x.value)
   444	
   445	        parent_item = v.ListItem(
   446	            value=str(folder.parent),
   447	            children=[
   448	                v.ListItemAction(
   449	                    children=[
   450	                        v.Icon(
   451	                            color=self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["color"][v.theme.dark],
   452	                            children=[self.ICON_STYLE["PARENT"]["icon"]],
   453	                        )
   454	                    ]
   455	                ),
   456	                v.ListItemContent(
   457	                    children=[v.ListItemTitle(children=[f".. /{folder.parent.stem}"])]
   458	                ),
   459	            ],
   460	        )
   461	
   462	        folder_list.extend(file_list)
   463	        folder_list.insert(0, parent_item)
   464	
   465	        return folder_list
   466	
   467	    def _on_reload(self, *args) -> None:
   468	
   469	        # force the update of the current folder
   470	        self._change_folder()
   471	
   472	        return

But x ≠ 0, and for x = -1, y = x = -1, but the original equation requires y ≠ -x². For x = -1, y = -1, then -x² = -1, so y = -1 ≠ -x² = -1? No, y = -1 equals -x² when x = -1, since x² = 1. Therefore, for x = -1, y = -1 is not allowed. Therefore, x = y = -1 is excluded, so the substitution x = y would be invalid for x = -1.

Therefore, for x = y ≠ -1, f(x) = -x would give a contradiction in the equation. Therefore, f(x) = -x is not a solution.

Therefore, so far, the only solution seems to be f(x) = x. To confirm, let's check if another substitution works. Let's set y = x^4 - x². Then the equation becomes f(x² + x^4 - x²) = f(x)^2 + f(x(x^4 - x²))/f(x). Simplify LHS: f(x^4) = f(x)^2 + f(x^5 - x³)/f(x). If f(x) = x, then LHS: x^4. RHS: x² + (x^5 - x³)/x = x² + x^4 - x² = x^4. So it works. If f(x) = x is the only solution, this holds.

Alternatively, suppose there's a function such that f(x) = x for some x and different elsewhere. But given the equation, it's likely that f(x) must be x for all x. Let's try to see if we can prove that f(x) = x.

Suppose we assume that f is differentiable. Though the problem doesn't state this, maybe we can assume differentiability for the sake of exploration. Then, from the original equation, differentiate both sides with respect to y. The left side derivative is f’(x² + y). The right side derivative is [f(xy)/f(x)]’ with respect to y. Since f(x) is treated as a constant with respect to y, the derivative is [f’(xy) * x]/f(x). Therefore, f’(x² + y) = (x / f(x)) f’(xy).

If f(x) = x, then f’(x) = 1, and the equation becomes 1 = (x / x) * 1 ⇒ 1 = 1. Which holds.

If there is another function, say f(x) = kx. Let's see. Then f’(x) = k. Then left side derivative: k. Right side derivative: (x / (k x)) * k = (1/k) * k = 1. Therefore, k = 1. Hence, k=1 is the only possibility. Hence, f(x) = x.

This suggests that under differentiability, f(x) = x is the only solution. But since the problem doesn't specify differentiability, we need a more general proof.

Let’s revisit the substitution x = 1. We have f(y + 1) = c² + f(y)/c, where c = f(1). Let’s consider for y = 1, we get f(2) = c² + f(1)/c = c² + c/c = c² + 1. Similarly, y = 2: f(3) = c² + f(2)/c = c² + (c² + 1)/c = c² + c + 1/c. If c = 1, then f(2) = 2, f(3) = 3, etc., which fits f(y) = y. If c ≠ 1, then the values would be different. For instance, if c = 2, then f(2) = 4 + 1 = 5, f(3) = 4 + (5)/2 = 4 + 2.5 = 6.5, which doesn't correspond to a simple function. But we need this to hold for all real numbers y, not just integers. Therefore, if c ≠ 1, the function would have to follow a non-linear pattern which is hard to maintain for all real numbers. Hence, likely c must be 1, leading to f(y) = y.

Another approach: let's set y = x^2 - x² = 0. Wait, no, y = 0 is not allowed. Alternatively, set y = t - x² where t is a non-zero real number. Then, the equation becomes f(t) = f(x)^2 + f(x(t - x²))/f(x). Let me set t = x(t - x²). Wait, but that's recursive. Maybe set t = x². Then x(t - x²) = x(x² - x²) = 0, which is invalid.

Alternatively, let’s set t = 1. Then f(1) = f(x)^2 + f(x(1 - x²))/f(x). Which is the same as before. If we can find x such that x(1 - x²) = 1. For example, solve x - x³ = 1 ⇒ x³ - x + 1 = 0. Not sure if real solution exists. Let's see. The equation x³ - x + 1 = 0. Let’s check x = -1: (-1)^3 - (-1) + 1 = -1 +1 +1 =1 ≠0. x=0: 0 -0 +1=1≠0. x=1:1 -1 +1=1≠0. There's a real root between -2 and -1. Let me check x = -1.5: (-3.375) - (-1.5) +1= -3.375 +1.5 +1= -0.875. Still negative. x=-1.3: (-2.197) - (-1.3)+1= -2.197 +2.3≈0.103. So there's a root around x≈-1.32. Therefore, there exists an x such that x(1 - x²) =1. For such an x, we have f(1) = f(x)^2 + f(1)/f(x). Let’s denote f(x) = a, then equation becomes c = a² + c/a. Multiply by a: a c = a³ + c. Rearranged: a³ - a c + c = 0. Factor: a³ - a c + c = a(a² - c) + c. Not obvious. If we suppose that a = 1, then 1 - c + c =1=0? No. If a = c, then c³ - c² + c =0 ⇒ c(c² -c +1)=0. Since c ≠0, c² -c +1=0, which has no real solutions. Therefore, no solution here unless maybe another value.

Alternatively, if there's a solution with a = something else, but this is getting too convoluted. Perhaps the only solution is f(x) = x.

Given all these attempts and substitutions leading to f(x) = x as the only viable solution, I think that f(x) = x is the only function that satisfies the given equation. Therefore, the solution is f(x) = x.

   f(1 + y) = f(1)^2 + \frac{f(y)}{f(1)}
   \]
   Denote \( f(1) = c \). Then the equation simplifies to:
   \[
   f(y + 1) = c^2 + \frac{f(y)}{c}
   \]

2. **Assume \( f \) is linear:**
   Suppose \( f(y) = ky + d \). Substituting into the equation:
   \[
   f(y + 1) = k(y + 1) + d = ky + k + d
   \]
   The right side becomes:
   \[
   c^2 + \frac{ky + d}{c} = c^2 + \frac{ky}{c} + \frac{d}{c}
   \]
   Equate coefficients:
   \[
   ky + k + d = c^2 + \frac{ky}{c} + \frac{d}{c}
   \]
   For the coefficients of \( y \):
   \[
   k = \frac{k}{c} \implies c = 1 \text{ (since } k \neq 0\text{)}
   \]
   For the constant terms:
   \[
   k + d = c^2 + \frac{d}{c} \implies k + d = 1 + d \implies k = 1
   \]
   Therefore, \( f(y) = y + d \).

3. **Determine \( d \):**
   Substitute \( f(y) = y + d \) back into the original equation:
   \[
   f(x^2 + y) = x^2 + y + d
   \]
   \[
   f(x)^2 + \frac{f(xy)}{f(x)} = (x + d)^2 + \frac{xy + d}{x + d}
   \]
   Simplify the right side:
   \[
   (x + d)^2 + \frac{xy + d}{x + d} = x^2 + 2xd + d^2 + \frac{xy + d}{x + d}
   \]
   For the equation to hold for all \( y \):
   \[
   x^2 + y + d = x^2 + 2xd + d^2 + \frac{xy + d}{x + d}
   \]
   Simplify further:
   \[
   y + d = 2xd + d^2 + \frac{xy + d}{x + d}
   \]
   Multiply both sides by \( x + d \):
   \[
   (y + d)(x + d) = (2xd + d^2)(x + d) + xy + d
   \]
   Expand and simplify:
   \[
   yx + yd + dx + d^2 = 2x^2d + 2xd^2 + d^2x + d^3 + xy + d
   \]
   Equate coefficients:
   \[
   yx + yd + dx + d^2 = xy + 2x^2d + 2xd^2 + d^2x + d^3 + d
   \]
   For the constant term:
   \[
   d^2 = d^3 + d \implies d^2(1 - d) = 0 \implies d = 0 \text{ (since } d \neq 1\text{)}
   \]
   Therefore, \( f(y) = y \).

4. **Verification:**
   Substitute \( f(x) = x \) back into the original equation:
   \[
   f(x^2 + y) = x^2 + y
   \]
   \[
   f(x)^2 + \frac{f(xy)}{f(x)} = x^2 + \frac{xy}{x} = x^2 + y
   \]
   Both sides are equal, confirming that \( f(x) = x \) is indeed a solution.

Thus, the only function \( f: X \to X \) that satisfies the given equation is:
\[
\boxed{f(x) = x}
\]<#sep#> is 15 (binary 1111).

for s in range(n):

    # Initialize DP for this starting point s.

    dp = [ [INF]*n for _ in range(1<<n) ]

    initial_mask = 1 << s
    dp[initial_mask][s] = 0 

    masks_order = []
    for mask in range(1<<n):
        cnt = bin(mask).count('1')
        masks_order.append( (cnt, mask) )

    masks_order.sort(key=lambda x: x[0])

    for (cnt, mask) in masks_order:
        for v in range(n):
            current_dist = dp[mask][v]
            if current_dist == INF:
                continue

            # Iterate through each possible outgoing edge from v to u.

            for u in range(n):
                if dist[v][u] != INF:
                    # Edge exists between v and u.

                    # Check whether u is not in mask.

                    if ( (mask & (1 << u)) == 0 ):
                        new_mask = mask | (1<<u)
                        new_d = current_dist + dist[v][u]
                        if new_d < dp[new_mask][u]:
                            dp[new_mask][u] = new_d 

    # After processing all masks, check if full mask can be closed.

    full_mask = (1<<n) -1 

    current_s_min = INF 

    # For each v in the full mask, which is all nodes.

    for v in range(n):

        if dp[full_mask][v] == INF:
            continue 

        # Check if there's an edge from v to s.

        if dist[v][s] != INF:

            total = dp[full_mask][v] + dist[v][s]
            if total < current_s_min:
                current_s_min = total 

    if current_s_min < min_total:
        min_total = current_s_min 

if min_total == INF:
    print(-1)
else:
    print(min_total)

import sys

def main():

    n,e = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())

    INF = float('inf')

    edges = [[] for _ in range(n)]

    dist = [[INF]*n for _ in range(n)]

    for _ in range(e):

        s,t,d = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())

        edges[s].append( (t, d) )

        dist[s][t] = d

    min_total = INF 

    for s in range(n):

        dp = [ [INF]*n for _ in range(1<<n) ]

        initial_mask = 1<<s 

        dp[initial_mask][s] =0 

        masks_order = [ ( bin(mask).count('1'), mask ) for mask in range(1<<n) ]

        masks_order.sort()

        for (cnt, mask) in masks_order:

            for v in range(n):

                current_dist = dp[mask][v]

                if current_dist == INF:

                    continue 

                for (u, d) in edges[v]:

                    if (mask & (1 <<u)) ==0:

                        new_mask = mask | (1<<u)

                        new_dist = current_dist +d 

                        if new_dist < dp[ new_mask ][u]:

                            dp[new_mask][u] = new_dist 

        # now process the full mask 

        full_mask = (1<<n)-1 

        current_min = INF 

        for v in range(n):

            if dp[full_mask][v] == INF: continue

            if dist[v][s] != INF:

                total = dp[full_mask][v] + dist[v][s]

                if total < current_min:

                    current_min = total 

        if current_min < min_total:

            min_total = current_min 

    if min_total == INF:

        print(-1)

    else:

        print( min_total )

if __name__ == "__main__":

    main()

To solve this problem, we need to find the shortest closed cycle in a directed graph that visits each vertex exactly once. This is essentially a variant of the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) for directed graphs. The solution involves using dynamic programming with bitmasking to efficiently explore all possible paths and track the shortest distances.

### Approach

1. **Input and Initialization**:
   - Read the graph details and build an adjacency list to store edges.
   - Initialize a distance matrix `dist` where `dist[v][u]` holds the distance from vertex `v` to `u`, or infinity if no direct edge exists.

2. **Dynamic Programming Setup**:
   - For each starting vertex `s`:
     - Initialize a DP table `dp` where `dp[mask][v]` represents the shortest path to vertex `v` with a visited vertex set represented by the bitmask `mask`.
     - The starting vertex `s` is initialized with distance 0, and all other entries are set to infinity.

3. **Processing Masks**:
   - Process all possible masks in order of increasing bit count. This ensures we build paths incrementally from smaller sets of visited vertices to larger ones.
   - For each mask and each vertex `v` in the current path, update the DP table by considering all outgoing edges from `v`.

4. **Checking for Valid Cycles**:
   - After processing all masks, check if there is a valid cycle starting and ending at `s` by verifying if there's an edge from the last vertex (in the full mask) back to `s`.

5. **Result Calculation**:
   - Track the minimum distance across all starting vertices. If no valid cycle exists, return -1.

### Solution Code

```python
import sys

def main():
    n, e = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
    INF = float('inf')
    edges = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    dist = [[INF] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    for _ in range(e):
        s, t, d = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        edges[s].append((t, d))
        dist[s][t] = d
    
    min_total = INF
    
    for s in range(n):
        dp = [[INF] * n for _ in range(1 << n)]
        initial_mask = 1 << s
        dp[initial_mask][s] = 0
        
        masks_order = [(bin(mask).count('1'), mask) for mask in range(1 << n)]
        masks_order.sort()
        
        for (cnt, mask) in masks_order:
            for v in range(n):
                current_dist = dp[mask][v]
                if current_dist == INF:
                    continue
                for (u, d) in edges[v]:
                    if not (mask & (1 << u)):
                        new_mask = mask | (1 << u)
                        new_dist = current_dist + d
                        if new_dist < dp[new_mask][u]:
                            dp[new_mask][u] = new_dist
        
        full_mask = (1 << n) - 1
        current_min = INF
        for v in range(n):
            if dp[full_mask][v] == INF:
                continue
            if dist[v][s] != INF:
                total = dp[full_mask][v] + dist[v][s]
                if total < current_min:
                    current_min = total
        
        if current_min < min_total:
            min_total = current_min
    
    print(-1 if min_total == INF else min_total)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Explanation

- **Dynamic Programming Table (`dp`)**: This table tracks the shortest path from the starting vertex to each vertex for all subsets of vertices (represented by bitmasks). Each entry `dp[mask][v]` holds the shortest path to vertex `v` using the vertices in the subset represented by `mask`.
  
- **Mask Processing Order**: By processing masks in order of increasing bit count, we ensure that paths are built incrementally. This allows us to consider smaller subsets of vertices before their supersets.

- **Edge Iteration**: For each vertex and its current path, we look at all outgoing edges to explore potential next vertices, updating the DP table accordingly.

- **Cycle Check**: After processing all possible subsets, we check if there's an edge back to the starting vertex from the last vertex in a full path (all vertices visited), ensuring the cycle's closure.

This approach efficiently explores all possible paths using dynamic programming and bitmasking, ensuring we find the shortest valid cycle or determine its impossibility.<#sep#> format/structure.

    73	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows.
    74	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.
    75	
    76	The full documentation is available `here <https://sepal-ui.readthedocs.io/en/latest/>`__ and demo apps can be launched on Heroku following these links:
    77

    79	-   `Panel style application <https://sepal-panel-app.herokuapp.com>`__

Wait, but how does this work? For example, suppose you have an area of all # except for two N's separated by a connected path of #. So from one N, the region would have to stop at the other N. Therefore, for each N, compute the maximum connected region which includes that N and all adjacent (through non-N cells), but cannot contain any other N.

Ah, so the connected component starting from the N, but treating Ns as blocked except for the original N. Therefore, when building the region for a particular N, other Ns are treated as walls, and the region is found by spreading from the N, not crossing through other N's, and forming the maximum possible connected region.

Alternatively, think of it as a graph where each cell (N or #) is considered, but edges can only be passed through in the 4 directions. For each N, the desired region is the set of all cells reachable from that N without passing through another N (i.e., excluding any paths that reach an N outside the current one). So, the algorithm is as follows:

The size of this region is the count of cells in it. The maximum value of (size -1) over all N's is the answer (since each region has exactly one N, so subtracting gives the number of hay cells, which is the desired output).

Therefore the plan is:

1. Iterate over each N in the grid.

2. For each such N's position, compute the size of the maximal region that can be formed containing it and all reachable # (without stepping into other Ns).

3. Take the maximum of (region size minus 1) for all such regions. That is the answer.

Wait, in the general formula for the area of the inscribed circle in a sector with radius R and angle θ, the area is π R² sin²(θ/2) / (1 + sin(θ/2))². For θ=120°, sin(θ/2)=sin(60°)=√3/2. So:

Area = π R² ( (√3/2)^2 ) / (1 + √3/2)^2 = π R² (3/4) / ( (2 + √3)/2 )^2 = π R² (3/4) / ( (4 + 4√3 + 3)/4 ) = π R² (3/4) * (4)/(7 +4√3 ) = π R² (3)/(7 +4√3 )

Multiply numerator and denominator by (7 -4√3 ):

Area = 3π R² (7 -4√3 ) / [ (7 +4√3)(7 -4√3 ) ] = 3π R² (7 -4√3 ) / (49 - 48 ) = 3π R² (7 -4√3 ) / 1 = 3π R² (7 -4√3 )

Wait, this gives Area = 21π R² -12√3 π R², which is the same as π R² (21 -12√3 ). So yes, same result. So regardless of the approach, we get the same area. Therefore, the answer is correct.

Therefore, the maximum area is π R² (21 -12√3 ). However, let me check this calculation again:

Wait, when we used the general formula:

Area = π R² sin²(θ/2) / (1 + sin(θ/2))²

For θ = 120°, sin(θ/2) = sin(60°) = √3/2.

Therefore, Area = π R² ( (√3/2)^2 ) / (1 + √3/2)^2 = π R² (3/4) / ( (2 + √3)/2 )^2 = π R² (3/4) / ( (4 + 4√3 + 3)/4 ) = π R² (3/4) * (4)/(7 +4√3 ) = π R² 3/(7 +4√3 )

Then rationalizing:

3/(7 +4√3 ) = 3(7 -4√3 ) / [ (7 +4√3)(7 -4√3 ) ] = 3(7 -4√3 ) / (49 - 48 ) = 3(7 -4√3 )

Therefore, Area = π R² *3(7 -4√3 ) = π R² (21 -12√3 )

Same as before. So this confirms the result. Therefore, the answer is correct.

Hence, the maximum area is π R² (21 -12√3 ).

import sys
g=[[*s.strip()]for s in sys.stdin]
R,C=len(g),len(g[0])if g else 0
m=0
for i in range(R):
 for j in range(C):
    if g[i][j] == 'N':
        v={(i,j)}; q=[[i,j]]; c=1
        while q:
            x,y=q.pop(0)
            for dx,dy in [[-1,0],[1,0],[0,-1],[0,1]]:
                nx,ny=x+dx,y+dy
                if 0<=nx<R and 0<=ny<C and (g[nx][ny] == "#" or (nx,ny)==(i,j)) and (nx,ny) not in v:
                    v.add((nx,ny))
                    c +=1
                    q += [[nx,ny]]
        if c-1>m:
            m=c-1
print(m)

</think>To find the maximum area of a circle inscribed in a sector with radius \( R \) and a central angle of 120 degrees, we follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Geometry:**
   - The inscribed circle is tangent to both radii of the sector and the arc of the sector.
   - The center of the inscribed circle lies on the angle bisector of the sector, which is 60 degrees from each radius.

2. **Define Variables:**
   - Let \( d \) be the distance from the origin (center of the sector) to the center of the inscribed circle.
   - Let \( r \) be the radius of the inscribed circle.

3. **Distance Relationships:**
   - The distance from the center of the inscribed circle to each radius is \( r \).
   - Since the center is on the angle bisector, we have:
     \[
     r = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} d
     \]

4. **Tangency Condition:**
   - The inscribed circle is tangent to the arc of the sector, so the distance from the origin to the center of the inscribed circle plus the radius of the inscribed circle equals the radius of the sector:
     \[
     d + r = R
     \]

5. **Substitute and Solve for \( d \):**
   - Substitute \( r = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} d \) into the tangency condition:
     \[
     d + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} d = R
     \]
     \[
     d \left(1 + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) = R
     \]
     \[
     d = \frac{2R}{2 + \sqrt{3}}
     \]

6. **Solve for \( r \):**
   - Substitute \( d \) back into \( r = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} d \):
     \[
     r = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cdot \frac{2R}{2 + \sqrt{3}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}R}{2 + \sqrt{3}}
     \]
   - Rationalize the denominator:
     \[
     r = \frac{\sqrt{3}R(2 - \sqrt{3})}{(2 + \sqrt{3})(2 - \sqrt{3})} = \frac{\sqrt{3}R(2 - \sqrt{3})}{1} = R(2\sqrt{3} - 3)
     \]

7. **Calculate the Area of the Inscribed Circle:**
   - The area of the inscribed circle is \( \pi r^2 \):
     \[
     \text{Area} = \pi (R(2\sqrt{3} - 3))^2 = \pi R^2 (2\sqrt{3} - 3)^2
     \]
   - Expand the square:
     \[
     (2\sqrt{3} - 3)^2 = (2\sqrt{3})^2 - 2 \cdot 2\sqrt{3} \cdot 3 + 3^2 = 12 - 12\sqrt{3} + 9 = 21 - 12\sqrt{3}
     \]

Thus, the maximum area of the inscribed circle is:

\[
\boxed{(21 - 12\sqrt{3}) \pi R^2}

First, the standard form of a parabola is y = ax² + bx + c, but it's often easier to work with the vertex form. So maybe I should complete the square to convert the given equation into vertex form. Let me try that.

The given equation is y = x² + 20x + 20. To complete the square, I'll take the coefficient of x², which is 1, so that's straightforward. The x terms: x² + 20x. To complete the square, take half of 20, which is 10, square it to get 100. So add and subtract 100:

y = (x² + 20x + 100) - 100 + 20
y = (x + 10)² - 80

So the vertex form is y = (x + 10)² - 80. That means the vertex of the parabola is at (-10, -80). Since the coefficient of (x + 10)² is positive, the parabola opens upwards.

Now, the next step is to find the focus and directrix of the parabola because the latus rectum and latus nextum are defined in terms of them. For a parabola in vertex form y = a(x - h)² + k, the focus is at (h, k + 1/(4a)) and the directrix is the line y = k - 1/(4a).

In our case, a = 1 (since the coefficient is 1), h = -10, and k = -80. So the focus should be at (h, k + 1/(4a)) = (-10, -80 + 1/(4*1)) = (-10, -80 + 0.25) = (-10, -79.75). Similarly, the directrix is y = k - 1/(4a) = -80 - 0.25 = -80.25.

Okay, so focus at (-10, -79.75) and directrix y = -80.25. Now, the latus nextum is a horizontal line segment (parallel to directrix) twice as long, so length 2. Where could that be? Since the parabola is symmetric, there should be two points on either side of the axis of symmetry (x = -10) such that the horizontal distance between them is 2. Since the parabola is opening upwards, a horizontal line at some y-coordinate will intersect the parabola at two points, and if the distance between those two points is 2, that's a latus nextum. But how high or low is this line?

Wait, but the problem says "the latus nextum is defined as the line segment parallel to the directrix, with endpoints on the parabola, that is twice as long as the latus rectum." So, the latus rectum is 1 unit long, so the latus nextum is 2 units long. Since the directrix is horizontal, the latus nextum is a horizontal segment. Since the parabola is symmetric, the line y = c will intersect the parabola at points (a, c) and (-20 - a, c) because the axis of symmetry is x = -10. Wait, maybe not. Wait, the parabola is y = (x + 10)^2 - 80. So solving for x when y = c:

(x + 10)^2 - 80 = c
(x + 10)^2 = c + 80
x + 10 = ±√(c + 80)
x = -10 ± √(c + 80)

Therefore, the two points on the horizontal line y = c are at x = -10 + √(c + 80) and x = -10 - √(c + 80). The distance between them is 2√(c + 80). So we need this distance to be 2 (for latus nextum, which is twice the latus rectum, which is 1). Wait, hold on: the latus rectum is length 1, so the latus nextum is length 2. So for the latus nextum, 2√(c + 80) = 2, so √(c + 80) = 1, so c + 80 = 1, so c = -79. Wait, but the latus rectum is at y = -79.75. Hmm, c = -79 is higher up (since the parabola opens upwards). Wait, but then the horizontal line y = -79 would intersect the parabola at x = -10 ±1, so distance between them is 2. But the latus rectum was at y = -79.75. So the latus nextum is at y = -79, which is higher up than the latus rectum. Is that correct?

Wait, but according to the problem statement, the latus rectum is the one passing through the focus, which is at (-10, -79.75). So the latus nextum is another horizontal line, parallel to directrix, with endpoints on the parabola, twice as long. So the length is 2, so we need to find a horizontal line y = c where the intersection points with the parabola are 2 units apart. As above, this happens when 2√(c + 80) = 2, so √(c + 80) = 1, so c = -79. So the latus nextum is at y = -79, with endpoints at x = -10 ±1, so (-9, -79) and (-11, -79). Let me verify if those points are on the parabola:

For x = -9: y = (-9)^2 + 20*(-9) + 20 = 81 - 180 + 20 = -79. Yes. For x = -11: y = (-11)^2 + 20*(-11) + 20 = 121 - 220 + 20 = -79. Correct. So the latus nextum is the segment from (-11, -79) to (-9, -79), length 2. So that's correct.

Wait, but just to be thorough, could there be another horizontal line lower down (i.e., with a lower y-coordinate) where the segment is length 2? The parabola opens upwards, so as we go lower in y, the horizontal lines would intersect the parabola only if y is ≥ the minimum value. Wait, the minimum y is at the vertex, which is -80. So for c ≥ -80, the equation y = c will intersect the parabola. The distance between the intersection points on y = c is 2√(c + 80). For c approaching -80 from above, the distance approaches 0. For c increasing, the distance increases. So the only horizontal lines with endpoints on the parabola that are length 2 would be at y = -79. But wait, if c = -79.75, which is the focus, the length is 1, as we saw. So if we take a c higher than that, we get length 2 at c = -79. So that's the latus nextum.

Therefore, the latus rectum is the segment from (-10.5, -79.75) to (-9.5, -79.75), and the latus nextum is from (-11, -79) to (-9, -79). Now, the problem is to compute the greatest possible distance between a point on the latus rectum and a point on the latus nextum.

So we need to find the maximum distance between any point on the latus rectum and any point on the latus nextum. Let's parameterize both line segments.

First, the latus rectum: it's the horizontal segment from (-10.5, -79.75) to (-9.5, -79.75). So any point on the latus rectum can be written as (x1, -79.75) where x1 ranges from -10.5 to -9.5.

The latus nextum is the horizontal segment from (-11, -79) to (-9, -79). Any point on it can be written as (x2, -79) where x2 ranges from -11 to -9.

We need to find the maximum distance between (x1, -79.75) and (x2, -79). The distance squared between these points is (x2 - x1)^2 + (-79 - (-79.75))^2 = (x2 - x1)^2 + (0.75)^2.

To maximize the distance, we need to maximize |x2 - x1| because the vertical component is fixed at 0.75. The maximum |x2 - x1| occurs when x2 is as far left as possible and x1 as far right as possible, or vice versa. Let's check the ranges:

x1 is between -10.5 and -9.5.

x2 is between -11 and -9.

So the maximum x2 - x1 would be when x2 is maximum (x2 = -9) and x1 is minimum (x1 = -10.5). Then x2 - x1 = -9 - (-10.5) = 1.5.

Alternatively, the minimum x2 - x1 would be when x2 is minimum (x2 = -11) and x1 is maximum (x1 = -9.5). Then x2 - x1 = -11 - (-9.5) = -1.5. The absolute value is 1.5 in both cases. So the maximum |x2 - x1| is 1.5. Therefore, the maximum distance is sqrt((1.5)^2 + (0.75)^2).

Calculating that: (1.5)^2 = 2.25, (0.75)^2 = 0.5625. Sum is 2.25 + 0.5625 = 2.8125. Square root of 2.8125 is sqrt(2.8125). Let's compute that:

2.8125 = 45/16. Because 2.8125 = 2 + 13/16 = 45/16. sqrt(45/16) = (3*sqrt(5))/4. Wait:

Wait, 45 is 9*5, so sqrt(45) = 3*sqrt(5). So sqrt(45/16) = 3*sqrt(5)/4. 3/4 is 0.75, sqrt(5) is approximately 2.236, but we need the exact value. Therefore, sqrt(45/16) = (3√5)/4. Let me verify:

(3√5)/4 squared is 9*5/16 = 45/16, which is 2.8125. Correct.

So the maximum distance is 3√5/4. But let me check the coordinates again to make sure.

Wait, another way: the horizontal separation between the right end of latus rectum (-9.5, -79.75) and the left end of latus nextum (-11, -79). The horizontal distance is |-9.5 - (-11)| = 1.5. The vertical distance is |-79.75 - (-79)| = 0.75. So the distance between these two points is sqrt(1.5^2 + 0.75^2) = sqrt(2.25 + 0.5625) = sqrt(2.8125) = sqrt(45/16) = 3√5/4. Similarly, the distance between (-10.5, -79.75) and (-9, -79) is |-10.5 - (-9)| = |-1.5| = 1.5, same vertical difference. So same distance. Therefore, the maximum distance is 3√5/4.

But wait, hold on: 3√5/4. Let me compute that: 3 times approximately 2.236 is 6.708, divided by 4 is about 1.677. But the problem says "greatest possible distance", which is sqrt( (1.5)^2 + (0.75)^2 ). Let me check another possibility. Are there any points on the latus rectum and latus nextum that could be further apart?

Wait, maybe I should consider the endpoints. The latus rectum endpoints are (-10.5, -79.75) and (-9.5, -79.75). The latus nextum endpoints are (-11, -79) and (-9, -79). So the two farthest points would be between (-10.5, -79.75) and (-11, -79), or between (-9.5, -79.75) and (-9, -79). Wait, but the first pair: (-10.5, -79.75) and (-11, -79). The horizontal distance is |-10.5 - (-11)| = 0.5. Vertical is 0.75. So distance sqrt(0.5² + 0.75²) = sqrt(0.25 + 0.5625) = sqrt(0.8125) ≈ 0.901. Which is less than 1.677.

Similarly, (-9.5, -79.75) to (-9, -79): horizontal distance 0.5, vertical 0.75, same as above.

Alternatively, perhaps some points not at the endpoints? But we saw that the maximum horizontal separation is 1.5. So the points that are farthest apart horizontally would be at x2 = -9 and x1 = -10.5 (distance 1.5) and x2 = -11 and x1 = -9.5 (distance 1.5). Both give the same maximum horizontal separation. Therefore, the maximum distance is sqrt( (1.5)^2 + (0.75)^2 ).

Which is sqrt(2.25 + 0.5625) = sqrt(2.8125). As a fraction, 2.8125 is 45/16. So sqrt(45/16) = (3√5)/4. That's the exact value.

So the greatest possible distance is 3√5/4. Let me make sure I didn't make any mistakes in the steps. The key points were converting the equation to vertex form, finding focus and directrix, then determining the latus rectum and latus nextum. The maximum distance between points on those segments comes from the maximum horizontal separation since the vertical separation is fixed. Calculating that gives the answer. I think that's all correct.

**Final Answer**
The greatest possible distance is \boxed{\dfrac{3\sqrt{5}}{4}}.
</think>To solve the problem of finding the greatest possible distance between a point on the latus rectum and a point on the latus nextum of the parabola given by \( y = x^2 + 20x + 20 \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Convert the equation to vertex form:**
   \[
   y = x^2 + 20x + 20
   \]
   Completing the square:
   \[
   y = (x + 10)^2 - 100 + 20 = (x + 10)^2 - 80
   \]
   So, the vertex form is:
   \[
   y = (x + 10)^2 - 80
   \]
   The vertex is at \((-10, -80)\).

2. **Determine the focus and directrix:**
   For a parabola \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), the focus is at \((h, k + \frac{1}{4a})\) and the directrix is \( y = k - \frac{1}{4a} \).
   Here, \( a = 1 \), \( h = -10 \), and \( k = -80 \). Therefore:
   \[
   \text{Focus} = \left( -10, -80 + \frac{1}{4} \right) = \left( -10, -79.75 \right)
   \]
   \[
   \text{Directrix} = y = -80 - \frac{1}{4} = -80.25
   \]

3. **Find the latus rectum:**
   The latus rectum is a horizontal line segment through the focus with length \( 4p \), where \( p = \frac{1}{4} \). Thus, the length is \( 1 \).
   The endpoints of the latus rectum are:
   \[
   \left( -10 - \frac{1}{2}, -79.75 \right) = \left( -10.5, -79.75 \right)
   \]
   \[
   \left( -10 + \frac{1}{2}, -79.75 \right) = \left( -9.5, -79.75 \right)
   \]

4. **Find the latus nextum:**
   The latus nextum is a horizontal line segment parallel to the directrix and twice as long as the latus rectum, so its length is \( 2 \).
   To find the y-coordinate of the latus nextum, we solve for \( y = c \) such that the distance between the intersection points with the parabola is 2:
   \[
   (x + 10)^2 - 80 = c
   \]
   \[
   (x + 10)^2 = c + 80
   \]
   \[
   x + 10 = \pm \sqrt{c + 80}
   \]
   \[
   x = -10 \pm \sqrt{c + 80}
   \]
   The distance between the intersection points is:
   \[
   2\sqrt{c + 80} = 2 \implies \sqrt{c + 80} = 1 \implies c + 80 = 1 \implies c = -79
   \]
   The endpoints of the latus nextum are:
   \[
   \left( -10 - 1, -79 \right) = \left( -11, -79 \right)
   \]
   \[
   \left( -10 + 1, -79 \right) = \left( -9, -79 \right)
   \]

5. **Calculate the greatest distance:**
   The greatest distance between a point on the latus rectum and a point on the latus nextum<#sep#>,0可以 go to the right到 đến row2列0.0 1 → N, so no.

R,C=len(g), len(g[0]) if g else 0

m=0

 for j in range(C):

    if g[i][j] == 'N':

        v={(i,j)}

        q=[[i,j]]

        c=1

        while q:

            x,y=q.pop(0)

            for dx,dy in [[0,1],[1,0],[0,-1],[-1,0]]:

                nx, ny =x+dx, y+dy

 explanatory version first then optimize:

import sys

g=[[*l.strip()]for l in sys.stdin]

R,C=len(g),len(g[0])if g else 0

m=0

for i in range(R):

 for j in range(C):

    if g[i][j]== 'N':

        v,s={(i,j)},[(i,j)];c=1

        while s:

            x,y=s.pop(0)

            for dx,dy in(0,1),(1,0),(0,-1),(-1,0):

                nx=x+dx;ny=y+dy

                if -1<nx<R and -1< ny< C and (g[nx][ny]=='#'or(nx,ny)==(i,j)) and (nx,ny) not in v:

                    v.add((nx,ny));c+=1;s+=[[nx,ny]]

        m=max(m,c-1)

print(m)

This is shorter by using the directions as tuples, and combined the visited and queue.

 Also, initial uses v,s instead of initializing them separately.

 Now, let's count the characters.

But in code golf, we need to minimize spaces and use one line loops.

 Also, the condition:

 -1 < nx < R → which is equivalent to 0<=nx < R.

 similarly for ny.

 The tuple of directions can be written more concisely using complex literals.

但 using comma separated tuples is  good.

 Other optimizations:

- Initialize the variables with more concise '';

- Use the initial empty queue as s 시작하면 a list containing [i,j], but starting with [[i,j]] → s is the same as that.

    72	
    73	:code:`sepal_ui` is a lib designed to create elegant python based dashboard in the `SEPAL environment <https://sepal.io/>`__. It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows.
    74	By using this libraries, you'll ensure a robust and unified interface for your scripts and a easy and complete integration into the SEPAL dashboard of application.
    75	
    76	The full documentation is available `here <https://sepal-ui.readthedocs.io/en/latest/>`__ and demo apps can be launched on Heroku following these links:
    77	
    78	-   `Map style application <https://sepal-map-app.herokuapp.com>`__
    79	-   `Panel style application <https://sepal-panel-app.herokuapp.com>`__
    80	
    81	We are happy to receive feedback and we welcome any kind of contribution.

I've noticed a number of OEIS (On-line Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences) challenges since I joined the site. It seems only fair that we have a cops-and-robbers challenge that determines who is the master of online integer sequences, once and for all.
Edit: In order to prevent trivial answers, cops lose 1/2 point for each submission that is cracked. Additionally, for the purposes of this challenge, constant sequences are not allowed. This only applies to solutions posted after this edit.
Cops
Write a program or function that, given no input, deterministically prints any sequence from the OEIS. By deleting some subset of the characters, your program must be able to print a different OEIS sequence when run in the same language. The new sequence must be entirely new, not just the first by a different name or with a different offset. Neither sequence may be simply a repeated constant value.
You must provide the first function, along with the name of the OEIS sequence so correctness can be verified. It's OK if behavior gets questionable around your language's MAX_INT value or 256, whichever is larger.
Robbers
Delete characters from some Cop's submission such that your new program outputs any other sequence from the OEIS. Provide the new function along with the name of the new OEIS sequence. Here is a utility to make sure that your submission is valid (i.e. deletes characters without any funny business.  Doesn't check the sequence itself.)
It's in your best interest to delete as many characters from the Cop's submission as possible. If another robber (anyone except the Cop who authored the original program) comes along and finds a shorter solution that finds another different sequence, that robber steals your point. (Note that simply golfing off characters and printing the same sequence is not sufficient to steal the point.)
Rules & Scoring
If, after one week, nobody has been able to crack your solution, you can mark your solution as safe by providing the second program along with the name of the sequence it generates.
You get one point for each safe posting and one point for each submission you crack. Cops lose 1/2 point for each cracked submission. Note that another robber can steal your point from the cracked submission at any time by providing a shorter program that yields a different sequence.
Cops may only post one challenge per language, per person.
The player with the most points at 12:00 UTC on 7 July wins.
